[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I... I don't even really know where to start, to be perfectly honest with you. It's just that recently, I keep having these incredibly bizarre moments. And I mean bizarre. It's like... well, what brings me in is that my own house, or even my office where I've been doing taxes for, gosh, twenty years now—they just feel completely unreal to me. It's the strangest sensation. I'll be sitting there at my desk, looking at a spreadsheet or a client's W-2, and suddenly it's like I'm watching myself from across the room instead of actually being there in my own chair. It's like I'm a spectator to my own life, just hovering near the filing cabinets or looking down from the ceiling. And it's not just a passing thought, it's a physical, overwhelming feeling that nothing around me is solid or real, and I'm just disconnected from my own body. It's been so unsettling, and it happens when I'm just trying to make a cup of coffee in my own kitchen, too. I'll look at the mug and think, 'Am I really here doing this?' It's just... it's really starting to scare me, frankly.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced seeing or hearing things that aren't there when you're having these unreal moments?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. I mean, I'm not... I'm not hallucinating or anything. I don't see, you know, ghosts or hear voices whispering to me from the vents or anything like you see in the movies. It's just... it's exactly what I said, it's just this intense feeling of being disconnected. Everything I see is actually there—the coffee mug, the W-2s, the filing cabinets, the little plant my wife bought me for my desk that I keep forgetting to water—it's all physically there, and I know it's there. My eyes are working perfectly fine, my hearing is fine, except for a little ringing sometimes, but that's just from getting older, I think. But no, no voices, no visions. It's just that the reality of those things, the *feeling* of them being real and me being real while interacting with them, that's what vanishes. I'll be looking right at my computer screen, seeing the numbers clear as day, but it feels like I'm looking at a movie set of my office, not my actual office. So, no, I'm not seeing things that aren't there, I'm just... not feeling like I'm there with the things that *are* there, if that makes any sense at all. It's so hard to explain, but I definitely want to be clear that I'm not losing my mind in that way. No hallucinations. Just this... this floating, unreal sensation.\n\nDoctor: Do these feelings of unreality seem to happen more often when you're under a lot of stress or pressure?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's funny you ask that, because I've been trying to figure out a pattern myself. I mean, as a tax preparer, stress is practically in the job description, right? Especially around April, which, thank goodness, is behind us for now, but even still, there are always extensions and audits and people panicking about their quarterly payments. So, I'm used to pressure. I've handled pressure for twenty years. But these... these floating episodes, these out-of-body moments, they don't seem to care if I'm staring down a deadline or just, you know, watering the lawn on a Sunday afternoon. \n\nJust last week, for instance, I was sitting on the back patio. The weather was beautiful, seventy-two degrees, a nice little breeze. My wife, Susan, was inside making a salad, and I was just sitting there doing absolutely nothing. No spreadsheets, no clients calling, no pressure whatsoever. And then, out of nowhere, bam. I'm looking at the patio furniture, and it looks like a prop. I'm looking at my own hands resting on my knees, and they don't feel like my hands. It was just as intense as when I'm at the office. \n\nSo, to answer your question, no, I haven't really noticed them happening more when I'm stressed. It's almost more frustrating that way, to be honest. If it only happened when I was swamped with work, I could just say, 'Okay, I need to take a vacation, I need to step back.' But when it happens while I'm just trying to enjoy a quiet Sunday, or making that cup of coffee I mentioned earlier... it just feels completely random. And that randomness, that unpredictability, is almost more stressful than the tax season itself, because I never know when I'm suddenly going to feel like a ghost in my own life. I keep trying to track it, keep trying to find some trigger—did I sleep poorly? Did I drink too much caffeine? But so far, I just can't connect it to stress or pressure or anything else I can put my finger on. It just... happens.\n\nDoctor: How long do these episodes of feeling disconnected and unreal typically last?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's hard to put an exact stopwatch on it, because when you're in the middle of it, time just sort of... warps, I guess you could say. It's like asking how long a dream lasts when you're dreaming it. But if I had to really sit down and estimate, I'd say it varies, but it's never just a quick flash. It's not like a light switch flicking off and on. \n\nUsually, when it hits—like that time on the patio I just told you about, or when I'm at my desk and suddenly I'm floating by the filing cabinets watching myself—it settles in for a good while. I'd say maybe... twenty minutes? Sometimes half an hour? I remember one time last Tuesday, I was looking over a particularly messy set of deductions for a client who owns a bakery down on 4th Street, and it started. I looked at the clock on my computer monitor, and it was 2:15. I just sat there, feeling completely detached, like my hands weren't mine, like the whole office was just a cardboard set. I couldn't work. I just kind of stared, trying to ground myself, trying to feel real again. And by the time that foggy, out-of-body feeling finally started to lift and the room felt solid again, I looked back at the clock and it was almost 3:00. So that was a solid forty-five minutes of just... not being there. \n\nIt's exhausting, frankly. You wouldn't think just sitting there feeling unreal would take it out of you, but it does. When it finally passes, I feel like I've run a marathon, just drained. And the whole time it's happening, I'm just waiting for it to end, hoping someone doesn't walk into my office and try to have a normal conversation with me, because I don't know how I'd even respond when I feel like I'm watching the whole interaction from the ceiling. So, yeah, I'd say anywhere from twenty minutes to nearly an hour, usually. It just lingers.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any significant anxiety or fear when you're not having these feelings of unreality?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. And it's... it's exhausting in a completely different way than the floating feeling. It's like I can never just turn off, you know? Even when I'm not having one of those episodes where the world feels fake, I'm just so... on edge. Constantly. \n\nLike, even just being at home in Roanoke, where I've lived for fifteen years, where nothing bad has ever happened to me, I can't just relax. Susan will be watching TV in the living room, and I'll be sitting there, but I'm not really watching the show. I'm listening to everything else. Every single car that drives past our house, I'm tracking it in my head. If I hear a car door slam down the street, my heart jumps. I'm listening for footsteps on the porch, you know? Like, the mailman dropping off a package shouldn't make me feel like I need to brace myself, but it does. \n\nAnd it's not just at home, it's everywhere. If we go out to a restaurant, say, to grab dinner on a Friday night, I can't just sit with my back to the door anymore. I have to know exactly where the exits are, like I'm casing the joint or something. I'm constantly scanning the room, looking at the other people, just... waiting. It's this overwhelming, physical feeling that I need to be ready, like I have to be prepared for someone to come at me again, even though I know logically that I'm just sitting at an Olive Garden and nobody is going to attack me. But my body doesn't seem to know that. My brain just won't let my guard down, not even for a second. So, yes, the anxiety is definitely there, and it's just draining me dry. I can't sleep properly because every creak of the house wakes me up, and I can't just enjoy a quiet evening because I'm basically standing guard in my own living room. It's just... it's a lot.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any traumatic events in your past that might be contributing to your current feelings of anxiety and disconnection?\n\nPatient: Well, when you say \"past,\" I don't know if you mean, like, childhood trauma or a bad car accident from twenty years ago, because if so, no. I've had a pretty quiet, boring life, honestly. But if you mean recently... yes. Very recently, actually. In fact, I know exactly what started all of this, because I can pinpoint the exact day. \n\nThis whole thing—the feeling unreal, the jumping at every sound, all of it—it has been going on for exactly two weeks. Two weeks ago this past Wednesday, to be precise. I was just sitting in my office. Like I said before, I'm a tax preparer, and even though the main April rush is over, we still deal with a lot of stragglers, people dealing with audits or back taxes, things like that. So, I had this client come in. I won't use his name, obviously, professional confidentiality and all that, but he had gotten a notice from the IRS about a rather large penalty he owed. And I had the paperwork right there on my desk. I was trying to walk him through it calmly, trying to explain the tax codes and why he was being penalized, just doing my job, but he just... he didn't want to hear it. \n\nHe completely lost it. He slammed his hands down on my desk—knocked over my coffee mug, scattered my pens and highlighters everywhere, it was a huge mess—and he leaned right over my computer monitor and he threatened me. He looked me dead in the eye and made a very specific, very violent threat right there in my own office. My office! A place where I've worked safely, where I've helped people, for over a decade. I actually had to have the building manager come down, it was this whole terrifying ordeal. And ever since that exact moment, for these entire last two weeks, I just haven't been the same. It's like a switch got flipped the second he leaned over that desk, and I haven't been able to turn it back off. So, yes, that's what happened. Two weeks of this, all because of that one afternoon.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it difficult to maintain your relationships with family and friends due to these episodes and anxiety?\n\nPatient: Well, as far as Susan goes, she's been an absolute saint, really. I know I'm not exactly a joy to be around right now, what with the constant pacing and jumping out of my skin every time the ice maker drops a cube in the kitchen. And I haven't even tried to meet up with the guys for our usual Saturday morning golf game since all this happened, because I just can't bring myself to sit in the clubhouse and make small talk. \n\nBut honestly, doctor, where it's really, truly falling apart—where the wheels are just completely coming off the wagon—is at the office. My work relationships and my clients... it's a total disaster. You have to understand, I've prided myself for years on being meticulous. In tax preparation, you can't afford to be sloppy, you know? People's livelihoods are on the line. But these last two weeks? I've actually missed several client deadlines. Me! Missing deadlines! I had a corporate extension due last Thursday for a mid-sized landscaping firm we've represented for a decade, and I just completely blew right past it because I was too busy staring at my office door, just waiting for someone to burst in and attack me. \n\nIt's gotten so bad that I've actually stopped seeing people at the office altogether. I told Brenda, our front desk lady, to cancel all my in-person appointments indefinitely and just tell people I'm unavailable. I just... I don't trust myself right now. I really don't. I don't trust myself to handle these complex returns accurately when my brain feels like it's wrapped in cotton or floating outside my body half the day. And more than that, I just don't feel like I can keep the workplace safe right now. If another client comes in and gets aggressive, or even just raises their voice about a penalty they don't like, I don't know what I'd do. I'm so incredibly on edge, I feel like I'd either freeze up completely or just panic. I can't guarantee I could protect myself or Brenda. So I've just shut my door. I'm hiding out, ignoring the phone, pushing my clients away... it's just completely upended everything I've built.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific places or situations that you've been avoiding since the incident with the client in your office?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... no, not really. Not beyond what I was just saying about the office and the face-to-face client meetings. I haven't, you know, drawn up a map and crossed out whole sections of town or anything like that. I'm not refusing to leave my house. \n\nSusan and I still go to the Kroger to get our groceries, and I still run out to get gas for the car, and like I mentioned earlier, we still tried to go out to dinner at the Olive Garden. So I haven't stopped going to normal, everyday places. I'm not avoiding the bank or the post office or the hardware store. \n\nIt's just that... well, when I *am* in those places, I'm just so incredibly tense. It's not that I won't go into the grocery store, it's that when I'm in the produce aisle, I'm checking around the corners of the fruit displays to see who's walking up behind me. So I'm there, physically, but I'm not relaxed at all. But to answer your question directly, no, there's no specific place or building I'm completely steering clear of, except for sitting across a desk from a client. That's the only situation I've completely put a stop to. Everything else, I'm still doing... I'm just doing it while feeling like I'm about to be jumped. \n\nIt's exhausting, really, having to go about your normal routine when your brain is treating a trip to the pharmacy like you're walking into a trap. You just want to buy some toothpaste, but instead you're eyeing the guy in the next aisle because he's walking a little too fast. But yeah, as far as actually avoiding places? No, not really. Just the in-person work stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping at night since these episodes started?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's actually funny you ask that, because you would really think so, wouldn't you? With everything else going on, and how wound up I am during the day, you'd think I'd be tossing and turning all night. Susan actually brought that exact same thing up just the other morning while we were having our coffee. But, honestly... no. Sleep is about the only thing that hasn't been completely ruined by all this. \n\nWe have this routine, Susan and I, where we get into bed and watch a little bit of television—usually just the local news, maybe a rerun of a sitcom, nothing too heavy or dramatic. And I've always been a pretty solid sleeper. Even during the absolute peak of tax season, which, let me tell you, is notoriously stressful. I mean, by mid-April I'm usually pulling twelve, sometimes fourteen-hour days, staring at 1040s and complex corporate schedules until my eyes practically cross. But even then, once I shut off the bedside lamp, I'm out like a light. \n\nAnd surprisingly, that really hasn't changed. Even these last two weeks, I haven't had any trouble drifting off. I'm not waking up in a cold sweat, I'm not lying there for hours staring at the ceiling fan... I just sleep. I think maybe it's because I'm just so incredibly exhausted by the time the evening rolls around. Being so tense all day, constantly checking over my shoulder at the grocery store like I mentioned earlier, or jumping out of my skin every time the ice maker goes off... it just drains you. It physically and mentally drains you. So by the time 10:30 or 11:00 PM hits, my brain just kind of gives up and shuts down completely. So, no, I haven't noticed any issues with my sleep at all. The nights are actually perfectly fine. It's having to wake up and face the day that's the real problem.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Well, you know, it's funny you mention that. Susan actually brought up the exact same thing just the other day. We were sitting in the living room, and I was, you know, pacing back and forth like I always seem to be doing lately, just completely unable to sit still and watch the evening news with her. You just deal with the stress and keep moving forward.

I'll be sitting there at my desk, looking at a spreadsheet or a client's W-2, and suddenly it's like I'm watching myself from across the room instead of actually being there in my own chair. It's just that the reality of those things, the *feeling* of them being real and me being real while interacting with them, that's what vanishes. I'll be looking right at my computer screen, seeing the numbers clear as day, but it feels like I'm looking at a movie set of my office, not my actual office. So, no, I'm not seeing things that aren't there, I'm just... not feeling like I'm there with the things that *are* there, if that makes any sense at all. It's so hard to explain, but I definitely want to be clear that I'm not losing my mind in that way. No hallucinations. Just this... this floating, unreal sensation.\n\nDoctor: Do these feelings of unreality seem to happen more often when you're under a lot of stress or pressure?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's funny you ask that, because I've been trying to figure out a pattern myself. I mean, as a tax preparer, stress is practically in the job description, right? Especially around April, which, thank goodness, is behind us for now, but even still, there are always extensions and audits and people panicking about their quarterly payments. So, I'm used to pressure. I've handled pressure for twenty years. The weather was beautiful, seventy-two degrees, a nice little breeze. My wife, Susan, was inside making a salad, and I was just sitting there doing absolutely nothing. I'm looking at my own hands resting on my knees, and they don't feel like my hands. It was just as intense as when I'm at the office. \n\nSo, to answer your question, no, I haven't really noticed them happening more when I'm stressed. It's almost more frustrating that way, to be honest. If it only happened when I was swamped with work, I could just say, 'Okay, I need to take a vacation, I need to step back.' But when it happens while I'm just trying to enjoy a quiet Sunday, or making that cup of coffee I mentioned earlier... it just feels completely random. And that randomness, that unpredictability, is almost more stressful than the tax season itself, because I never know when I'm suddenly going to feel like a ghost in my own life. It's not like a light switch flicking off and on. \n\nUsually, when it hits—like that time on the patio I just told you about, or when I'm at my desk and suddenly I'm floating by the filing cabinets watching myself—it settles in for a good while. I'd say maybe... twenty minutes? Sometimes half an hour? I remember one time last Tuesday, I was looking over a particularly messy set of deductions for a client who owns a bakery down on 4th Street, and it started. I looked at the clock on my computer monitor, and it was 2:15. I just sat there, feeling completely detached, like my hands weren't mine, like the whole office was just a cardboard set. I couldn't work. I just kind of stared, trying to ground myself, trying to feel real again. And by the time that foggy, out-of-body feeling finally started to lift and the room felt solid again, I looked back at the clock and it was almost 3:00. So that was a solid forty-five minutes of just... not being there. \n\nIt's exhausting, frankly. You wouldn't think just sitting there feeling unreal would take it out of you, but it does. When it finally passes, I feel like I've run a marathon, just drained. And the whole time it's happening, I'm just waiting for it to end, hoping someone doesn't walk into my office and try to have a normal conversation with me, because I don't know how I'd even respond when I feel like I'm watching the whole interaction from the ceiling. So, yeah, I'd say anywhere from twenty minutes to nearly an hour, usually. It just lingers.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any significant anxiety or fear when you're not having these feelings of unreality?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. And it's... it's exhausting in a completely different way than the floating feeling. It's like I can never just turn off, you know? Even when I'm not having one of those episodes where the world feels fake, I'm just so... on edge. Susan will be watching TV in the living room, and I'll be sitting there, but I'm not really watching the show. I'm listening to everything else. Every single car that drives past our house, I'm tracking it in my head. If I hear a car door slam down the street, my heart jumps. I'm listening for footsteps on the porch, you know? If we go out to a restaurant, say, to grab dinner on a Friday night, I can't just sit with my back to the door anymore. I have to know exactly where the exits are, like I'm casing the joint or something. I'm constantly scanning the room, looking at the other people, just... waiting. It's this overwhelming, physical feeling that I need to be ready, like I have to be prepared for someone to come at me again, even though I know logically that I'm just sitting at an Olive Garden and nobody is going to attack me. But my body doesn't seem to know that. My brain just won't let my guard down, not even for a second. So, yes, the anxiety is definitely there, and it's just draining me dry. I can't sleep properly because every creak of the house wakes me up, and I can't just enjoy a quiet evening because I'm basically standing guard in my own living room. It's just... it's a lot.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any traumatic events in your past that might be contributing to your current feelings of anxiety and disconnection?\n\nPatient: Well, when you say \"past,\" I don't know if you mean, like, childhood trauma or a bad car accident from twenty years ago, because if so, no. I've had a pretty quiet, boring life, honestly. But if you mean recently... yes. Very recently, actually. Like I said before, I'm a tax preparer, and even though the main April rush is over, we still deal with a lot of stragglers, people dealing with audits or back taxes, things like that. So, I had this client come in. I won't use his name, obviously, professional confidentiality and all that, but he had gotten a notice from the IRS about a rather large penalty he owed. And I had the paperwork right there on my desk. I was trying to walk him through it calmly, trying to explain the tax codes and why he was being penalized, just doing my job, but he just... he didn't want to hear it. \n\nHe completely lost it. He slammed his hands down on my desk—knocked over my coffee mug, scattered my pens and highlighters everywhere, it was a huge mess—and he leaned right over my computer monitor and he threatened me. He looked me dead in the eye and made a very specific, very violent threat right there in my own office. My office! A place where I've worked safely, where I've helped people, for over a decade. I actually had to have the building manager come down, it was this whole terrifying ordeal. And ever since that exact moment, for these entire last two weeks, I just haven't been the same. It's like a switch got flipped the second he leaned over that desk, and I haven't been able to turn it back off. So, yes, that's what happened. Two weeks of this, all because of that one afternoon.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it difficult to maintain your relationships with family and friends due to these episodes and anxiety?\n\nPatient: Well, as far as Susan goes, she's been an absolute saint, really. People's livelihoods are on the line. But these last two weeks? I've actually missed several client deadlines. Me! Missing deadlines! I had a corporate extension due last Thursday for a mid-sized landscaping firm we've represented for a decade, and I just completely blew right past it because I was too busy staring at my office door, just waiting for someone to burst in and attack me. \n\nIt's gotten so bad that I've actually stopped seeing people at the office altogether. I told Brenda, our front desk lady, to cancel all my in-person appointments indefinitely and just tell people I'm unavailable. I just... I don't trust myself right now. I really don't. I don't trust myself to handle these complex returns accurately when my brain feels like it's wrapped in cotton or floating outside my body half the day. And more than that, I just don't feel like I can keep the workplace safe right now. If another client comes in and gets aggressive, or even just raises their voice about a penalty they don't like, I don't know what I'd do. I'm so incredibly on edge, I feel like I'd either freeze up completely or just panic. I can't guarantee I could protect myself or Brenda. So I've just shut my door. I'm hiding out, ignoring the phone, pushing my clients away... it's just completely upended everything I've built.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific places or situations that you've been avoiding since the incident with the client in your office?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... no, not really. Not beyond what I was just saying about the office and the face-to-face client meetings. I haven't, you know, drawn up a map and crossed out whole sections of town or anything like that. I'm not refusing to leave my house. \n\nSusan and I still go to the Kroger to get our groceries, and I still run out to get gas for the car, and like I mentioned earlier, we still tried to go out to dinner at the Olive Garden. So I haven't stopped going to normal, everyday places. I'm not avoiding the bank or the post office or the hardware store. \n\nIt's just that... well, when I *am* in those places, I'm just so incredibly tense. It's not that I won't go into the grocery store, it's that when I'm in the produce aisle, I'm checking around the corners of the fruit displays to see who's walking up behind me. So I'm there, physically, but I'm not relaxed at all. But to answer your question directly, no, there's no specific place or building I'm completely steering clear of, except for sitting across a desk from a client. That's the only situation I've completely put a stop to. Everything else, I'm still doing... I'm just doing it while feeling like I'm about to be jumped. \n\nIt's exhausting, really, having to go about your normal routine when your brain is treating a trip to the pharmacy like you're walking into a trap. You just want to buy some toothpaste, but instead you're eyeing the guy in the next aisle because he's walking a little too fast. But yeah, as far as actually avoiding places? No, not really. Just the in-person work stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping at night since these episodes started?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's actually funny you ask that, because you would really think so, wouldn't you? With everything else going on, and how wound up I am during the day, you'd think I'd be tossing and turning all night. Susan actually brought that exact same thing up just the other morning while we were having our coffee. But, honestly... no. Sleep is about the only thing that hasn't been completely ruined by all this. \n\nWe have this routine, Susan and I, where we get into bed and watch a little bit of television—usually just the local news, maybe a rerun of a sitcom, nothing too heavy or dramatic. I'm not waking up in a cold sweat, I'm not lying there for hours staring at the ceiling fan... I just sleep. I think maybe it's because I'm just so incredibly exhausted by the time the evening rolls around. Being so tense all day, constantly checking over my shoulder at the grocery store like I mentioned earlier, or jumping out of my skin every time the ice maker goes off... it just drains you. It physically and mentally drains you. So by the time 10:30 or 11:00 PM hits, my brain just kind of gives up and shuts down completely. So, no, I haven't noticed any issues with my sleep at all. The nights are actually perfectly fine. It's having to wake up and face the day that's the real problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you thought about talking to someone else, like a therapist or a support group, about what you're going through?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's funny you mention that. Susan actually brought up the exact same thing just the other day. We were sitting in the living room, and I was, you know, pacing back and forth like I always seem to be doing lately, just completely unable to sit still and watch the evening news with her. I don't know if I'm the type of guy who would do well sitting on a couch for an hour every week, digging into all my feelings. I've always been more of a \"put your head down and push through it\" kind of person, you know? That's how I got through college, that's how I built my tax practice from the ground up. You just deal with the stress and keep moving forward.\n\nBut now... I don't know. The idea of sitting in a room with other people, talking about all this... it just sounds exhausting. I just haven't even considered the therapy route yet.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. And I've always been a pretty solid sleeper. Even during the absolute peak of tax season, which, let me tell you, is notoriously stressful. It physically and mentally drains you. So by the time 10:30 or 11:00 PM hits, my brain just kind of gives up and shuts down completely. So, no, I haven't noticed any issues with my sleep at all. It's having to wake up and face the day that's the real problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you thought about talking to someone else, like a therapist or a support group, about what you're going through?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's funny you mention that. I've always been more of a \"put your head down and push through it\" kind of person, you know? That's how I got through college, that's how I built my tax practice from the ground up. You just deal with the stress and keep moving forward.\n\nBut now... I don't know. The idea of sitting in a room with other people, talking about all this... it just sounds exhausting. I'd probably spend the whole time watching the door, just like I do at the office. So, no, I haven't really looked into any of that. I figured I'd start here, with you, and see if there's some sort of medical explanation or, I don't know, a pill that can just make my brain stop feeling like it's constantly sounding an alarm. I just haven't even considered the therapy route yet.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your physical health since these episodes started?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... no, not really. Well, you know, it's... I mean, gosh, no. Absolutely not. I know why you have to ask that, of course I do. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, you know, it's funny you ask that, because Susan and I were just talking about this the other night. She was trying to get me to write down a statement for the police, just to have it all documented while it's fresh, you know? And I sat there with this yellow legal pad for a good forty-five minutes, just staring at the lines. 

I mean, I remember the beginning of it perfectly clearly. I remember him coming into the office, I remember the look on his face, I remember him crowding me, backing me up against my desk. I can still smell the stale coffee on his breath, honestly. It's burned into my brain. And I remember the aftermath, too. I remember the police being there, the flashing lights outside the window, the officer with the little notebook asking me questions while I was shaking like a leaf. 

But the middle part? The part right before I actually grabbed the phone to call 911? It's just... gone. It's a complete blank. I know he was yelling, I know he was saying things, but if you put a gun to my head right now, I couldn't tell you what those words were. I've tried. Lord knows I've laid awake in bed at two in the morning, staring at the ceiling, trying to replay the whole thing like a movie in my head to see if I can catch what I missed. But it just skips. It goes from him looming over me and my heart hammering in my throat, to me suddenly having the phone receiver in my hand and dialing. 

It's the strangest feeling, missing a chunk of time like that. It makes you feel like you're losing your mind a little bit. You start second-guessing yourself. Did he actually threaten to hit me, or did I just assume he was going to? Did he say he was going to come back, or did I imagine that? The police asked me what his exact threats were, and I just... I couldn't tell them. I felt like an idiot. Here I am, a guy who makes his living keeping track of every single decimal point and receipt for hundreds of clients, and I can't even remember the most terrifying three minutes of my own life. It's incredibly frustrating, it really is. No matter how hard I try to pull it up, it's just a black hole.

    "role": "system",

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, I... I don't even really know where to start, to be perfectly honest with you. It's just that recently, I keep having these incredibly bizarre moments. And I mean bizarre. It's like... well, what brings me in is that my own house, or even my office where I've been doing taxes for, gosh, twenty years now—they just feel completely unreal to me. It's the strangest sensation. I'll be sitting there at my desk, looking at a spreadsheet or a client's W-2, and suddenly it's like I'm watching myself from across the room instead of actually being there in my own chair. It's like I'm a spectator to my own life, just hovering near the filing cabinets or looking down from the ceiling. And it's not just a passing thought, it's a physical, overwhelming feeling that nothing around me is solid or real, and I'm just disconnected from my own body. It's been so unsettling, and it happens when I'm just trying to make a cup of coffee in my own kitchen, too. I'll look at the mug and think, 'Am I really here doing this?' It's just... it's really starting to scare me, frankly.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced seeing or hearing things that aren't there when you're having these unreal moments?\n\nPatient: No, no, nothing like that. I mean, I'm not... I'm not hallucinating or anything. I don't see, you know, ghosts or hear voices whispering to me from the vents or anything like you see in the movies. It's just... it's exactly what I said, it's just this intense feeling of being disconnected. Everything I see is actually there—the coffee mug, the W-2s, the filing cabinets, the little plant my wife bought me for my desk that I keep forgetting to water—it's all physically there, and I know it's there. My eyes are working perfectly fine, my hearing is fine, except for a little ringing sometimes, but that's just from getting older, I think. But no, no voices, no visions. It's just that the reality of those things, the *feeling* of them being real and me being real while interacting with them, that's what vanishes. I'll be looking right at my computer screen, seeing the numbers clear as day, but it feels like I'm looking at a movie set of my office, not my actual office. So, no, I'm not seeing things that aren't there, I'm just... not feeling like I'm there with the things that *are* there, if that makes any sense at all. It's so hard to explain, but I definitely want to be clear that I'm not losing my mind in that way. No hallucinations. Just this... this floating, unreal sensation.\n\nDoctor: Do these feelings of unreality seem to happen more often when you're under a lot of stress or pressure?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's funny you ask that, because I've been trying to figure out a pattern myself. I mean, as a tax preparer, stress is practically in the job description, right? Especially around April, which, thank goodness, is behind us for now, but even still, there are always extensions and audits and people panicking about their quarterly payments. So, I'm used to pressure. I've handled pressure for twenty years. But these... these floating episodes, these out-of-body moments, they don't seem to care if I'm staring down a deadline or just, you know, watering the lawn on a Sunday afternoon. \n\nJust last week, for instance, I was sitting on the back patio. The weather was beautiful, seventy-two degrees, a nice little breeze. My wife, Susan, was inside making a salad, and I was just sitting there doing absolutely nothing. No spreadsheets, no clients calling, no pressure whatsoever. And then, out of nowhere, bam. I'm looking at the patio furniture, and it looks like a prop. I'm looking at my own hands resting on my knees, and they don't feel like my hands. It was just as intense as when I'm at the office. \n\nSo, to answer your question, no, I haven't really noticed them happening more when I'm stressed. It's almost more frustrating that way, to be honest. If it only happened when I was swamped with work, I could just say, 'Okay, I need to take a vacation, I need to step back.' But when it happens while I'm just trying to enjoy a quiet Sunday, or making that cup of coffee I mentioned earlier... it just feels completely random. And that randomness, that unpredictability, is almost more stressful than the tax season itself, because I never know when I'm suddenly going to feel like a ghost in my own life. I keep trying to track it, keep trying to find some trigger—did I sleep poorly? Did I drink too much caffeine? But so far, I just can't connect it to stress or pressure or anything else I can put my finger on. It just... happens.\n\nDoctor: How long do these episodes of feeling disconnected and unreal typically last?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's hard to put an exact stopwatch on it, because when you're in the middle of it, time just sort of... warps, I guess you could say. It's like asking how long a dream lasts when you're dreaming it. But if I had to really sit down and estimate, I'd say it varies, but it's never just a quick flash. It's not like a light switch flicking off and on. \n\nUsually, when it hits—like that time on the patio I just told you about, or when I'm at my desk and suddenly I'm floating by the filing cabinets watching myself—it settles in for a good while. I'd say maybe... twenty minutes? Sometimes half an hour? I remember one time last Tuesday, I was looking over a particularly messy set of deductions for a client who owns a bakery down on 4th Street, and it started. I looked at the clock on my computer monitor, and it was 2:15. I just sat there, feeling completely detached, like my hands weren't mine, like the whole office was just a cardboard set. I couldn't work. I just kind of stared, trying to ground myself, trying to feel real again. And by the time that foggy, out-of-body feeling finally started to lift and the room felt solid again, I looked back at the clock and it was almost 3:00. So that was a solid forty-five minutes of just... not being there. \n\nIt's exhausting, frankly. You wouldn't think just sitting there feeling unreal would take it out of you, but it does. When it finally passes, I feel like I've run a marathon, just drained. And the whole time it's happening, I'm just waiting for it to end, hoping someone doesn't walk into my office and try to have a normal conversation with me, because I don't know how I'd even respond when I feel like I'm watching the whole interaction from the ceiling. So, yeah, I'd say anywhere from twenty minutes to nearly an hour, usually. It just lingers.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any significant anxiety or fear when you're not having these feelings of unreality?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, actually. And it's... it's exhausting in a completely different way than the floating feeling. It's like I can never just turn off, you know? Even when I'm not having one of those episodes where the world feels fake, I'm just so... on edge. Constantly. \n\nLike, even just being at home in Roanoke, where I've lived for fifteen years, where nothing bad has ever happened to me, I can't just relax. Susan will be watching TV in the living room, and I'll be sitting there, but I'm not really watching the show. I'm listening to everything else. Every single car that drives past our house, I'm tracking it in my head. If I hear a car door slam down the street, my heart jumps. I'm listening for footsteps on the porch, you know? Like, the mailman dropping off a package shouldn't make me feel like I need to brace myself, but it does. \n\nAnd it's not just at home, it's everywhere. If we go out to a restaurant, say, to grab dinner on a Friday night, I can't just sit with my back to the door anymore. I have to know exactly where the exits are, like I'm casing the joint or something. I'm constantly scanning the room, looking at the other people, just... waiting. It's this overwhelming, physical feeling that I need to be ready, like I have to be prepared for someone to come at me again, even though I know logically that I'm just sitting at an Olive Garden and nobody is going to attack me. But my body doesn't seem to know that. My brain just won't let my guard down, not even for a second. So, yes, the anxiety is definitely there, and it's just draining me dry. I can't sleep properly because every creak of the house wakes me up, and I can't just enjoy a quiet evening because I'm basically standing guard in my own living room. It's just... it's a lot.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any traumatic events in your past that might be contributing to your current feelings of anxiety and disconnection?\n\nPatient: Well, when you say \"past,\" I don't know if you mean, like, childhood trauma or a bad car accident from twenty years ago, because if so, no. I've had a pretty quiet, boring life, honestly. But if you mean recently... yes. Very recently, actually. In fact, I know exactly what started all of this, because I can pinpoint the exact day. \n\nThis whole thing—the feeling unreal, the jumping at every sound, all of it—it has been going on for exactly two weeks. Two weeks ago this past Wednesday, to be precise. I was just sitting in my office. Like I said before, I'm a tax preparer, and even though the main April rush is over, we still deal with a lot of stragglers, people dealing with audits or back taxes, things like that. So, I had this client come in. I won't use his name, obviously, professional confidentiality and all that, but he had gotten a notice from the IRS about a rather large penalty he owed. And I had the paperwork right there on my desk. I was trying to walk him through it calmly, trying to explain the tax codes and why he was being penalized, just doing my job, but he just... he didn't want to hear it. \n\nHe completely lost it. He slammed his hands down on my desk—knocked over my coffee mug, scattered my pens and highlighters everywhere, it was a huge mess—and he leaned right over my computer monitor and he threatened me. He looked me dead in the eye and made a very specific, very violent threat right there in my own office. My office! A place where I've worked safely, where I've helped people, for over a decade. I actually had to have the building manager come down, it was this whole terrifying ordeal. And ever since that exact moment, for these entire last two weeks, I just haven't been the same. It's like a switch got flipped the second he leaned over that desk, and I haven't been able to turn it back off. So, yes, that's what happened. Two weeks of this, all because of that one afternoon.\n\nDoctor: Are you finding it difficult to maintain your relationships with family and friends due to these episodes and anxiety?\n\nPatient: Well, as far as Susan goes, she's been an absolute saint, really. I know I'm not exactly a joy to be around right now, what with the constant pacing and jumping out of my skin every time the ice maker drops a cube in the kitchen. And I haven't even tried to meet up with the guys for our usual Saturday morning golf game since all this happened, because I just can't bring myself to sit in the clubhouse and make small talk. \n\nBut honestly, doctor, where it's really, truly falling apart—where the wheels are just completely coming off the wagon—is at the office. My work relationships and my clients... it's a total disaster. You have to understand, I've prided myself for years on being meticulous. In tax preparation, you can't afford to be sloppy, you know? People's livelihoods are on the line. But these last two weeks? I've actually missed several client deadlines. Me! Missing deadlines! I had a corporate extension due last Thursday for a mid-sized landscaping firm we've represented for a decade, and I just completely blew right past it because I was too busy staring at my office door, just waiting for someone to burst in and attack me. \n\nIt's gotten so bad that I've actually stopped seeing people at the office altogether. I told Brenda, our front desk lady, to cancel all my in-person appointments indefinitely and just tell people I'm unavailable. I just... I don't trust myself right now. I really don't. I don't trust myself to handle these complex returns accurately when my brain feels like it's wrapped in cotton or floating outside my body half the day. And more than that, I just don't feel like I can keep the workplace safe right now. If another client comes in and gets aggressive, or even just raises their voice about a penalty they don't like, I don't know what I'd do. I'm so incredibly on edge, I feel like I'd either freeze up completely or just panic. I can't guarantee I could protect myself or Brenda. So I've just shut my door. I'm hiding out, ignoring the phone, pushing my clients away... it's just completely upended everything I've built.\n\nDoctor: Are there any specific places or situations that you've been avoiding since the incident with the client in your office?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... no, not really. Not beyond what I was just saying about the office and the face-to-face client meetings. I haven't, you know, drawn up a map and crossed out whole sections of town or anything like that. I'm not refusing to leave my house. \n\nSusan and I still go to the Kroger to get our groceries, and I still run out to get gas for the car, and like I mentioned earlier, we still tried to go out to dinner at the Olive Garden. I'm not avoiding the bank or the post office or the hardware store. \n\nIt's just that... well, when I *am* in those places, I'm just so incredibly tense. It's not that I won't go into the grocery store, it's that when I'm in the produce aisle, I'm checking around the corners of the fruit displays to see who's walking up behind me. So I'm there, physically, but I'm not relaxed at all. But to answer your question directly, no, there's no specific place or building I'm completely steering clear of, except for sitting across a desk from a client. That's the only situation I've completely put a stop to. Everything else, I'm still doing... I'm just doing it while feeling like I'm about to be jumped. \n\nIt's exhausting, really, having to go about your normal routine when your brain is treating a trip to the pharmacy like you're walking into a trap. You just want to buy some toothpaste, but instead you're eyeing the guy in the next aisle because he's walking a little too fast. But yeah, as far as actually avoiding places? No, not really. Just the in-person work stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping at night since these episodes started?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's actually funny you ask that, because you would really think so, wouldn't you? Susan actually brought that exact same thing up just the other morning while we were having our coffee. But, honestly... no. I mean, by mid-April I'm usually pulling twelve, sometimes fourteen-hour days, staring at 1040s and complex corporate schedules until my eyes practically cross. It physically and mentally drains you. So by the time 10:30 or 11:00 PM hits, my brain just kind of gives up and shuts down completely. So, no, I haven't noticed any issues with my sleep at all. The nights are actually perfectly fine. It's having to wake up and face the day that's the real problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you thought about talking to someone else, like a therapist or a support group, about what you're going through?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's funny you mention that. Susan actually brought up the exact same thing just the other day. We were sitting in the living room, and I was, you know, pacing back and forth like I always seem to be doing lately, just completely unable to sit still and watch the evening news with her. I don't know if I'm the type of guy who would do well sitting on a couch for an hour every week, digging into all my feelings. I've always been more of a \"put your head down and push through it\" kind of person, you know? That's how I got through college, that's how I built my tax practice from the ground up. You just deal with the stress and keep moving forward.\n\nBut now... I don't know. The idea of sitting in a room with other people, talking about all this... it just sounds exhausting. I couldn't do that to her, and I couldn't do that to the life we've built. I want to fix this. I want to figure out how to turn off this constant alarm bell ringing in my head so I can just go back to being me. So, no, I haven't had any thoughts like that at all. Just the in-person work stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping at night since these episodes started?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's actually funny you ask that, because you would really think so, wouldn't you? With everything else going on, and how wound up I am during the day, you'd think I'd be tossing and turning all night. Susan actually brought that exact same thing up just the other morning while we were having our coffee. But, honestly... no. Sleep is about the only thing that hasn't been completely ruined by all this. \n\nWe have this routine, Susan and I, where we get into bed and watch a little bit of television—usually just the local news, maybe a rerun of a sitcom, nothing too heavy or dramatic. And I've always been a pretty solid sleeper. Even during the absolute peak of tax season, which, let me tell you, is notoriously stressful. I mean, by mid-April I'm usually pulling twelve, sometimes fourteen-hour days, staring at 1040s and complex corporate schedules until my eyes practically cross. But even then, once I shut off the bedside lamp, I'm out like a light. \n\nAnd surprisingly, that really hasn't changed. Even these last two weeks, I haven't had any trouble drifting off. I'm not waking up in a cold sweat, I'm not lying there for hours staring at the ceiling fan... I just sleep. I think maybe it's because I'm just so incredibly exhausted by the time the evening rolls around. Being so tense all day, constantly checking over my shoulder at the grocery store like I mentioned earlier, or jumping out of my skin every time the ice maker goes off... it just drains you. It physically and mentally drains you. So by the time 10:30 or 11:00 PM hits, my brain just kind of gives up and shuts down completely. So, no, I haven't noticed any issues with my sleep at all. The nights are actually perfectly fine. It's having to wake up and face the day that's the real problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you thought about talking to someone else, like a therapist or a support group, about what you're going through?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's funny you mention that. Susan actually brought up the exact same thing just the other day. We were sitting in the living room, and I was, you know, pacing back and forth like I always seem to be doing lately, just completely unable to sit still and watch the evening news with her. And she said, \"Honey, maybe you need to go talk to somebody about this.\" And I just... I don't know. \n\nI mean, I'm here talking to you, obviously, which took a lot for me to finally agree to do. But as far as a therapist goes, or sitting in a circle with a bunch of strangers in some kind of support group? I just haven't really given it any serious thought. I don't know if I'm the type of guy who would do well sitting on a couch for an hour every week, digging into all my feelings. I've always been more of a \"put your head down and push through it\" kind of person, you know? That's how I got through college, that's how I built my tax practice from the ground up. You just deal with the stress and keep moving forward.\n\nBut now... I don't know. The idea of sitting in a room with other people, talking about all this... it just sounds exhausting. And frankly, with how on edge I am right now, the thought of being in a room full of strangers, even if they are going through similar things, just makes my chest tighten up. I'd probably spend the whole time watching the door, just like I do at the office. So, no, I haven't really looked into any of that. I figured I'd start here, with you, and see if there's some sort of medical explanation or, I don't know, a pill that can just make my brain stop feeling like it's constantly sounding an alarm. I just haven't even considered the therapy route yet.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your physical health since these episodes started?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean... no, not really. Not in the sense of, you know, getting sick or catching a cold or anything like that. I haven't had a fever, I haven't been coughing. My blood pressure might be up, I suppose, but I haven't actually checked it. Susan keeps telling me I should dig the monitor out of the closet—we bought one of those cuff things a few years ago when my doctor said my numbers were creeping up a little bit, but I haven't used it in ages. \n\nBut as far as actual, noticeable changes to my physical health? No. I mean, my stomach is in knots half the time, and like I said, I'm just completely exhausted by the end of the day from being so tense. My shoulders ache, my neck is stiff... but that's just from the stress of it all, right? That's just from walking around with my shoulders up around my ears all day long, waiting for the other shoe to drop. \n\nI haven't lost any weight, I haven't gained any weight. My appetite is pretty much the same. I still eat breakfast, still have my sandwich for lunch, still eat dinner with Susan. I might eat a little faster now, just trying to get it over with so I can get back to... well, I don't know what I'm getting back to, honestly, just pacing around the house, I guess. But no, I haven't noticed any real physical health problems. It's all just... up here, in my head, making my body feel like it's running a marathon every single day. But no actual illnesses or anything like that, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of harming yourself since these episodes started?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's... I mean, gosh, no. Absolutely not. I know why you have to ask that, of course I do. You see things on the news, you hear stories about people who just snap under the pressure of whatever they're going through, and I suppose it's a standard question you have to ask everybody who sits in that chair. But for me? No. Never. \n\nI mean, look, am I miserable right now? Yes. Am I exhausted from constantly looking over my shoulder and feeling like my heart is going to pound right out of my chest every time the mail carrier drops a package on the porch? Absolutely. It's an awful way to live, these past couple of weeks. It really is. It drains the life right out of you. But hurting myself? No. That hasn't even crossed my mind for a split second. \n\nI have Susan, you know? We've been married for almost thirty years, and she relies on me. And I have my practice. I've spent decades building up my client base, and people depend on me to get their taxes done right, especially now with all the changing regulations. I couldn't just... check out. I couldn't do that to her, and I couldn't do that to the life we've built. The whole reason I'm even here, sitting in this office and talking to you when I'd much rather be at my desk finishing up the quarterly estimates, is because I want to get back to normal. I want to fix this. I want to figure out how to turn off this constant alarm bell ringing in my head so I can just go back to being me. So, no, I haven't had any thoughts like that at all. I just want the anxiety to stop, I don't want everything else to stop.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding any specific activities or situations that remind you of the threatening client incident?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's... I mean, obviously, I'm not going back to my office. Not right now, anyway. I just can't. Susan has been going in for me to pick up the mail and bring home the files I absolutely have to get done, but even just seeing her bring those folders into the house makes my stomach do a flip. \n\nBut as far as specific activities or situations... I don't know. I guess I haven't really been out of the house enough to know what I'm avoiding, you know? I haven't been to the grocery store, I haven't been to the hardware store... I usually go to the driving range on Sunday mornings with a couple of guys from the Rotary Club, but I skipped that the last two weeks. I just told them I was backed up with work, which isn't entirely a lie, given how hard it is for me to concentrate right now. \n\nBut am I avoiding those things because they remind me of... of him? Of what happened? I don't think so. I don't think the driving range reminds me of an angry client threatening me. I think I'm just avoiding everything because I'm so keyed up. I just want to stay in my house, where I know the doors are locked and I can see who is pulling into the driveway. \n\nI guess... well, I did have Susan cancel the landscaping service for the rest of the month. They usually come on Tuesdays, and they have these big, loud leaf blowers and mowers, and the guys are always walking right up past my office window to get to the backyard. And the thought of just... unexpected people walking past the window, making all that noise, not knowing who they are or what they're doing... I just couldn't handle it. I told Susan I'd mow the lawn myself, even though I haven't done it in five years. So I guess that's something. But as for specific things that remind me of the incident itself? I don't know. I'm just trying to avoid anything that makes me feel exposed, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems with your memory since the threatening incident with your client?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's funny you ask that, because Susan and I were just talking about this the other night. She was trying to get me to write down a statement for the police, just to have it all documented while it's fresh, you know? And I sat there with this yellow legal pad for a good forty-five minutes, just staring at the lines. \n\nI mean, I remember the beginning of it perfectly clearly. I remember him coming into the office, I remember the look on his face, I remember him crowding me, backing me up against my desk. I can still smell the stale coffee on his breath, honestly. It's burned into my brain. And I remember the aftermath, too. I remember the police being there, the flashing lights outside the window, the officer with the little notebook asking me questions while I was shaking like a leaf. \n\nBut the middle part? The part right before I actually grabbed the phone to call 911? It's just... gone. It's a complete blank. I know he was yelling, I know he was saying things, but if you put a gun to my head right now, I couldn't tell you what those words were. I've tried. Lord knows I've laid awake in bed at two in the morning, staring at the ceiling, trying to replay the whole thing like a movie in my head to see if I can catch what I missed. But it just skips. It goes from him looming over me and my heart hammering in my throat, to me suddenly having the phone receiver in my hand and dialing. \n\nIt's the strangest feeling, missing a chunk of time like that. It makes you feel like you're losing your mind a little bit. You start second-guessing yourself. Did he actually threaten to hit me, or did I just assume he was going to? Did he say he was going to come back, or did I imagine that? The police asked me what his exact threats were, and I just... I couldn't tell them. I felt like an idiot. Here I am, a guy who makes his living keeping track of every single decimal point and receipt for hundreds of clients, and I can't even remember the most terrifying three minutes of my own life. It's incredibly frustrating, it really is. No matter how hard I try to pull it up, it's just a black hole.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

    "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Before? Oh, heavens no. Never before. I mean, I'm an accountant, for crying out loud. I deal in hard facts, spreadsheets, things you can add up and verify. I've always been a very grounded, practical kind of guy. I've never been one for, you know, out-of-body experiences or anything like that. 

But now... honestly, doctor, this is exactly what brings me in here to see you. I keep having these moments—and it's so hard to even describe without sounding like I'm losing my marbles—where my own house, or even my office when Susan convinced me to go back just to grab those critical files, just feels entirely unreal. 

It's like... it's like I'm watching myself from across the room instead of actually being there. I'll be sitting on the sofa in the living room, the exact same sofa I've sat on to watch the evening news for a decade, and suddenly it's like I'm standing over by the bookshelf, just looking at this middle-aged man sitting there. I can see my hands resting on my knees, but I don't really feel them. Susan will be talking to me, asking if I want chicken or pasta for dinner, and her voice sounds like it's coming from underwater, or through a long tunnel. 

And the room itself... it's just bizarre. The walls, the furniture, the photos on the mantle—it all suddenly looks like a fake movie set. Like none of it is mine, like it's just a simulation or something. I've lived in that house for twenty years, I helped build the deck out back, I know every creak in the floorboards, but in those moments, I feel completely detached from it all. I've never experienced anything remotely like this in my entire fifty-four years on this earth until that day with the client. It's terrifying, it really is, feeling like you're completely disconnected from your own life and just watching yourself exist from a distance.

I told Susan I'd mow the lawn myself, even though I haven't done it in five years. So I guess that's something. But as for specific things that remind me of the incident itself? I don't know. I'm just trying to avoid anything that makes me feel exposed, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems with your memory since the threatening incident with your client?\n\nPatient: Well, you know, it's funny you ask that, because Susan and I were just talking about this the other night. I know he was yelling, I know he was saying things, but if you put a gun to my head right now, I couldn't tell you what those words were. I've tried. Lord knows I've laid awake in bed at two in the morning, staring at the ceiling, trying to replay the whole thing like a movie in my head to see if I can catch what I missed. But it just skips. It goes from him looming over me and my heart hammering in my throat, to me suddenly having the phone receiver in my hand and dialing. \n\nIt's the strangest feeling, missing a chunk of time like that. It makes you feel like you're losing your mind a little bit. You start second-guessing yourself. Did he actually threaten to hit me, or did I just assume he was going to? Did he say he was going to come back, or did I imagine that? The police asked me what his exact threats were, and I just... I couldn't tell them. I felt like an idiot. Here I am, a guy who makes his living keeping track of every single decimal point and receipt for hundreds of clients, and I can't even remember the most terrifying three minutes of my own life. It's incredibly frustrating, it really is. No matter how hard I try to pull it up, it's just a black hole.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever have any feelings of unreality or disconnection from your body before the incident with the threatening client?\n\nPatient: Before? Oh, heavens no. Never before. I mean, I'm an accountant, for crying out loud. I deal in hard facts, spreadsheets, things you can add up and verify. I've always been a very grounded, practical kind of guy. I've never been one for, you know, out-of-body experiences or anything like that. \n\nBut now... honestly, doctor, this is exactly what brings me in here to see you. I keep having these moments—and it's so hard to even describe without sounding like I'm losing my marbles—where my own house, or even my office when Susan convinced me to go back just to grab those critical files, just feels entirely unreal. \n\nIt's like... it's like I'm watching myself from across the room instead of actually being there. I'll be sitting on the sofa in the living room, the exact same sofa I've sat on to watch the evening news for a decade, and suddenly it's like I'm standing over by the bookshelf, just looking at this middle-aged man sitting there. I can see my hands resting on my knees, but I don't really feel them. Susan will be talking to me, asking if I want chicken or pasta for dinner, and her voice sounds like it's coming from underwater, or through a long tunnel. \n\nAnd the room itself... it's just bizarre. The walls, the furniture, the photos on the mantle—it all suddenly looks like a fake movie set. Like none of it is mine, like it's just a simulation or something. I've lived in that house for twenty years, I helped build the deck out back, I know every creak in the floorboards, but in those moments, I feel completely detached from it all. I've never experienced anything remotely like this in my entire fifty-four years on this earth until that day with the client. It's terrifying, it really is, feeling like you're completely disconnected from your own life and just watching yourself exist from a distance."
